Despite being the world's third-largest aviation market, India's 
air travel penetration remains below that of major economies, 
providing substantial headroom. For India, the total passengers 
divided by total population (air travel per capita) was 0.29 as of 
calendar year 2025 compared to 1.08 in China and 5.59 in the 
US, which reflects significant untapped potential across India's 
large population base. Rising incomes, an increasing middle 
class, urbanisation, better regional connectivity and continued 
airport investment are expected to increase adoption and 
support long-term traffic growth. 
In FY 2025-26, Indian Air Travel Showed Resilience Amid 
Operational and Geopolitical Disruptions
In the current financial year, India’s air passenger traffic 
demonstrated strong resilience, consistently rebounding 
after operational and geopolitical disruptions. Traffic was 
impacted by airline-related and geopolitical disruptions 
during the year. However, traffic rebounded after each event, 
reflecting underlying strength and steadiness of passenger 
traffic demand in India. For example, October and November 
2025 saw a year-on-year increase in traffic, underscoring the 
strength of demand despite disruptions.India China USA

India’s Favourable Demographics and Rising Incomes to 
Support Passenger Traffic Growth 
Demand fundamentals remain robust, backed by favourable 
demographics, rising incomes and growing business and 
leisure travel.
Airport Infrastructure Investments and Growing Fleet Plans 
are Driving Supply-Side Expansion 
India's aviation sector has seen significant supply-side 
expansion, driven by rapid infrastructure development, a 
growing fleet and increasing route networks. Government 
initiatives and private investments have together built a strong 
foundation for sustained long-term traffic growth.Business and leisure travel
Personal travel (holidays, leisure, recreation) continues to rise, 
while India’s growing role as a business hub is increasing both 
domestic and international business travel
Rising urbanisation
India’s urban population is set to grow from 27.9%9 in 
2001 to 37.2%9 by 2030 thereby expanding the base of air 
travel-ready consumers
Young population
India has the world’s largest young population, with a median 
age of 28.1 years against a global median of 30.4. This working-
age majority, comprising those aged 25 to 54, makes up the 
bulk of the country’s airline passengers, a base widened 
further by the reach of low-cost carriersFleet Expansion
	 India’s fleet has nearly doubled in the past decade 
reaching more than 800 aircraft as of 2026
	 Indian airlines have placed orders for around 2,400 
aircraft, which is about three times the current capacity 
	 Low-Cost Carriers (LCCs) are democratising air travel in 
India by making it accessible to a broader segment of 
the population. LCC share in domestic passenger traffic 
increased to 85%10 in 2026 compared to 71%10 in 2019
Airport Infrastructure and investments
	 Number of airports in India have increased from 7410 
in 2014 to 16310 in 2025, with a target to increase to 
350-40010 by 2047
	 Airport investment projected to surpass H 1,000-1,100 
billion9 between fiscals 2026 and 2030, significantly 
higher than the H 750-810 billion9 estimated 
between fiscals 2021 and 2025
	 Greenfield and brownfield projects are underway, 
and new airports at Navi Mumbai and Noida (Jewar) 
have commenced operations alongside expansion 
at existing airports including Bengaluru, Hyderabad, 
Chennai and GuwahatiRising incomes
Steady income growth across corporate, rural and government 
sectors continues to drive domestic passenger traffic. India’s 
per capita income grew at a 6.6%9 Compound Annual Growth

Indian Airport Travel QSR Sector
Airport retail has become a fast-growing part of India's non-
aeronautical airport revenues, mainly comprising food & beverage, 
lounges, duty-free and convenience-led retail. The market is 
estimated at H  205-215 billion in FY 2025-26, with Travel QSR and 
Lounges together accounting for a 35% share, underscoring their 
growing importance within the airport ecosystem. 
Within this ecosystem, Travel QSR (Quick Service Restaurants) 
comprises F&B outlets at airports and other travel hubs, such 
as fast-food restaurants, cafés, food courts, bars, takeaway 
formats and bakeries catering to travellers seeking quick, 
convenient and high-quality dining in transit.
Indian Airport Travel QSR Sector to Grow at Strong Pace 
Airport food and beverage (F&B) consumption, driven by 
Travel QSR outlets based at airports, has evolved from a largely 
functional “grab a snack before boarding” category into an 
experience-led dining proposition. Airports in India are now 
positioning F&B as a core non-aeronautical revenue driver 
rather than a supporting amenity.
Indian Airport Travel QSR industry is estimated to grow at a 
18–20%9 CAGR between FY 2025-26 and FY 2029-30 (P), and 
further at a 16.5%-18.5%9 CAGR between FY 2029-30 (P) and 
FY 2034-35 (P), reaching H 170–190 billion.
Airport operators, Airport Travel QSR operators, F&B brands and 
passengers are key participants in the airport F&B value chain. 
Airport Travel QSR operators serve as an important link in the 
airport ecosystem by delivering convenient, high-quality dining 
experiences that enhance passenger satisfaction and non-
aeronautical revenue generation.

Indian Airport Travel QSR Sector Market Size (J Billion)
Premiumisation of Airport Travel QSR Offerings 
The Indian airport Travel QSR sector is experiencing a shift 
characterised by the growing involvement of premium and 
global brands. International food and beverage brands generally 
choose to enter Indian airports by partnering with established 
Travel QSR operators. Strong brand reputation and the ability 
to consistently deliver differentiated experiences within the 
complexities of the airport ecosystem, make them choose 
well-established Travel QSR operators. This preference reflects 
the distinct operational complexities of airport environments, 
making organised operators the favoured choice for global brands 
aiming for an airport presence. Additionally, this trend is backed 
by the increasing privatisation of Indian airports through the  
Public-Private Partnership (PPP) model. This model results in improved infrastructure, increased passenger traffic and an 
elevated focus on non-aeronautical revenues.
Indian Airport Lounge Market 
The Indian airport Lounge market has emerged as one of the 
fastest-growing vertical within airport retail. Traditionally 
reserved for business-class and premium travellers, Lounge 
access has broadened considerably through airline loyalty and 
frequent-flyer programmes, complimentary bank card offerings 
and lounge memberships.
The market is expected to grow to H  155 billion by FY 2034-
35 (P), reflecting a CAGR of 20.5%-22.5% over the next ten 
years. Today, Lounges offer high-quality F&B, comfortable 
seating, room to relax or work, and added facilities such as 
showers, wellness services and entertainment.

11CRISIL Report 2026Global Airport Lounges Market 
The global airport lounges market has evolved into a high-
growth, premium-driven business vertical within airport 
non-aeronautical revenues. The global airport lounge market 
has recorded a steady 7%11 CAGR by value between CY 2019 
and CY 2025 despite a dip in revenue for operators during 
COVID-19 impacted years. In the future, driven by increasing 
global passenger traffic and accessibility through credit card 
programmes, airline alliances and passengers’ appetite for 
quality experience, the global lounge market is expected 
to clock around 7.0%11 CAGR between CY 2025 and 2030 
(medium term) and 6.5%-7.5%11 CAGR between CY 2025 and 
CY 2035 (P) (long term). 
Rising Air Passenger Traffic:  Strong growth in domestic and international passenger traffic is increasing airport 
footfall, driving higher demand across airport retail, Travel QSR and lounge sectors
Expansion of Airport Infrastructure: Airport modernisation, terminal expansion and increasing operational airports 
are creating significant opportunities for retail, dining and lounge operators
Growth in Non-Aeronautical Revenues: Airports are increasingly focusing on retail, F&B and Lounge businesses to 
enhance non-aeronautical revenue contribution and improve passenger monetisation
Higher Passenger Dwell Time: Early arrivals, security procedures and operational delays are increasing passenger 
time spent within terminals, supporting higher retail and food consumption
Premiumisation of Travel: Rising disposable incomes and evolving consumer preferences are increasing demand for 
premium dining, branded retail and lounge experiences within airports
Credit Card-led Lounge Access Ecosystem:  Expansion of premium and mid-tier credit cards bundled with lounge 
access is significantly increasing lounge penetration and passenger utilisation
Semi-Captive Consumer Environment: Limited alternatives within airport terminals support higher spend per 
passenger across retail, food and lounge categories
Growth in Organised and Branded Formats:  Increasing preference for organised, branded and hygienic retail and 
dining formats is supporting higher penetration of organised airport operators
Technology and Digital Integration:  Digital ordering, contactless payments and technology-enabled passenger 
engagement are improving customer convenience and operational efficiency across airport retail ecosystemsCredit Card Ecosystem is one of the Key Growth Driver for Indian Airport Lounge Sector
The loyalty credit/debit card ecosystem has become the key driver of the Indian Lounge sector, with approximately 70–80% of 
lounge passenger traffic accessed through cards. Lounge access bundled with premium and mid-tier card offerings has transformed 
Lounge from niche premium services into scalable mass-premium formats, supporting higher utilisation, predictable traffic flows 
and stronger monetisation for operators. Despite this momentum, India's credit card penetration remains low at 4.62% against a 
global average of 22.26%, indicating significant headroom for further Lounge sector expansion.
Key Growth Drivers for Airport Travel QSR and Lounge Sectors in India
Growth is underpinned by rising passenger traffic volumes, improving airport infrastructure, low-but-expanding credit card 
penetration, airline frequent-flyer and premium-cabin programmes, the increase in lounge membership programmes, India’s 
emerging potential as a transit hub, and the premiumisation of Indian travellers.

Regional Growth Dynamics
Regional trends point to a clear shift towards emerging 
markets, with the Middle East & Africa and Asia-Pacific growing 
faster than the mature markets of North America and Europe.
According to the CRISIL Report 2026, Asia-Pacific (excluding 
China) has been the fastest-growing region in the global airport 
lounge market, with its share increasing from approximately 
13.7% in CY 2019 to 16.5%–17.0% by CY 2025E. The Middle East 
& Africa region has also expanded its presence, with its share 
rising from around 4.5% to 4.8%–5.3% over the same period.
This growth is driven by the emergence of Asia-Pacific and 
the Middle East as key global aviation and transit hubs. Strong 
passenger traffic growth, significant investments in airport 
infrastructure, and increasing demand for premium travel 
experiences are accelerating international and connecting 
traffic flows, thereby supporting higher lounge usage and 
ongoing premiumisation across these regions.

Indian Highway and Expressway QSR Industry
The Indian highway and expressway QSR industry is emerging as 
a significant long-term growth opportunity, supported by rapid 
expansion of road infrastructure, increasing intercity mobility 
and rising preference for organised roadside consumption 
formats. India's national highway network has expanded from 
approximately 91,000 km in FY 2013-14 to over 146,000 km 
in FY 2024-25, improving connectivity across major economic 
corridors and driving higher vehicle movement.
The Expressway Travel QSR industry has grown from nearly  
H4 billion in FY 2018-19 to H 15 billion in FY 2023-24 and is 
projected to reach H 265–285 billion by FY 2034-35 (P), reflecting 
a strong around 30% CAGR over the long term. Growth is 
expected to be driven by the continued development of access-
controlled expressways, increasing road travel for business and 
leisure purposes and rising demand for organised, hygienic and 
branded food and beverage offerings along highways.
Expressway Travel QSR Industry Size (J Billion)As highways evolve into structured travel corridors, designated 
wayside amenities and roadside retail hubs are creating new 
opportunities for organised Travel QSR operators. Supported 
by expanding highway infrastructure, rising intercity travel 
and increasing consumer preference for branded roadside 
dining experiences, the sector is expected to witness robust 
growth over the coming decade. While currently a relatively 
small contributor to the Company's overall business, the 
expressway travel QSR represents an attractive long-term 
growth opportunity.
Company Overview
TFS is the leading player in India's fast-growing airport Travel 
QSR and Lounge sectors by revenue in Fiscal 2026, with a 
System-wide market share of approximately 30% and 45% 
respectively, according to the CRISIL Report 2026.
The Company operates across 16 airports in India, 3 in Malaysia 
and 1 in Hong Kong, as of March 31, 2026. As Per the CRISIL 
Report 2026, 14 of its 16 Indian airports were among the 
country's 15 largest airports by passenger traffic in FY 2025-
26, including large airports like, Delhi, Mumbai, Bengaluru, 
Hyderabad, Kolkata and Chennai. 
The Company benefits from the combined experience of its 
Promoters, SSP Group plc and K-Hospitality. SSP is a FTSE 250 
company listed on the London Stock Exchange and is one of 
the leading operators in the Travel F&B sector globally. We 
have tailored SSP’s corporate governance standards to our 
business and draw on SSP’s relationships with global F&B 
brands in expanding our brand portfolio. K-Hospitality has over 
50 years of experience in the Indian F&B industry and owns/
invests in various hospitality and food services companies. 
K-Hospitality has developed culinary and operational know-
how, an understanding of Indian consumers and industry 
knowledge that TFS is able to benefit from. 
TFS operates long-term concessions for Travel QSR outlets and 
Lounges at key airports in India, spanning both Airports Authority 
of India (AAI) airports and those run by private operators. 
Alongside the long-term F&B concessions it operates directly, 
which form part of its standalone financials, the Company has 
grown its airport presence in India through strategic partnerships 
with Adani Airport Holdings Limited and GMR Airports Limited, 
held under subsidiary, joint venture and associate structures. 
These partnerships allow the Company to build its footprint at 
multiple airports while drawing on complementary strengths, 
operational expertise and established brand relationships. For 
its international operations in Malaysia and Hong Kong, the 
Company has partnered with SSP . System-wide presence refers 
to the combined network of TFS, its subsidiaries, associates and 
joint ventures across domestic and international markets.

The Travel QSR business remains the largest contributor to the 
Company's operations and is centred around offering quick 
service formats tailored to the needs of travellers, mainly within 
airports as well as at select highway sites to serve travellers’ 
demands for speed and convenience. We work closely with FY 2018-19430% CAGR

Lounge Business
The Airport Lounge business represents the Company's 
premium hospitality services that are designed to provide 
comfort and convenience to travellers at the airport. Lounges 
focus on providing a wide range of high-quality food options 
along with a comfortable seating arrangement for customers. 
Lounge services can be broadly bundled into food and beverage, 
space to relax or work, and ancillary facilities such as shower, 
wellness services, entertainment, etc. 
Our Lounge business comprised a total of 39 Lounges at a 
System-wide level, including 29 Lounges located in eleven 
airports in India, 8 Lounges located in three airports in Malaysia 
and 2 Lounges located in Hong Kong airport, as of March 31, 
2026. As per CRISIL Report 2026, our market share in the Indian 
airport Lounge sector was around 45% as of March 31, 2026, 
reinforcing our leadership position within one of the fastest-
growing sectors of airport hospitality. 
On the technology front, we continue our progress towards 
becoming a tech-enabled travel hospitality company. FY 2025-
26 was a landmark year, marked by the launch of Eliteassist 
Technology and Services Private Limited (EATS), our wholly 
owned subsidiary, which enables direct bank-to-lounge 
access as an integrated service. With end-to-end integration 
already deployed across multiple banks and card networks, 
the platform has stabilised well, and the early response has 
been encouraging. EATS remains a key enabler of customer 
access, convenience, engagement, and monetisation. Looking 
ahead, we are broadening the range of airport services offered 
through the platform and adding ancillary services to deepen 
customer engagement.

demanded agility, responsiveness and disciplined execution. Leveraging its agile operating model, TFS maintained uninterrupted 
service across locations through 24x7 operations, rapid deployment of essential food and beverage services, proactive menu 
rationalisation, and dynamic supply chain coordination. TFS ensured continuity of service, minimised passenger inconvenience, 
supported by real-time operational monitoring and cross-location manpower mobilisation, and reinforced the trust placed in it by 
airport operators, brand partners, and travellers alike.

During the year, the Company’s revenue mix saw a higher contribution from Travel QSR, which increased to 55.44% of total revenue 
from 51.68% in the previous year, primarily driven by the mobilisation of new Travel QSR outlets at airports. The contribution from 
Lounge services declined to 40.67% from 44.94%, mainly due to the de-consolidation of Semolina Kitchens Limited with effect 
from 15th October 2024. In FY 2024-25, revenue from operations included the consolidated revenues of Semolina Kitchens Limited, 
which were skewed towards its Lounge services operations, with lower contribution from Travel QSR. Additionally, management 
and other services contributed 3.89% of total revenue in FY 2025-26.
Margins and Operating Performance
Gross margin increased to 84.72% in FY 2025-26 from 81.74% in FY 2024-25 (adjusted basis), driven by procurement efficiencies. 
EBITDA margin increased to 39.36% in FY 2025-26 from 36.94% in FY 2024-25 (adjusted basis), supported by the improvement 
in gross margin and benefit of operating leverage. Share of profit from associates and joint ventures grew to H 663.98 million 
(FY 2024-25: H 497.53 million). Profit after tax grew 21.52% to H 4,523.17 million, with PAT margin increasing to 27.45% in the 
current year from 25.72% in the previous year (on an adjusted basis).
Balance Sheet Strength and Capital Efficiency
The Company continues to be debt-free as at March 31, 2026, with a consolidated cash and investment balance of H 8,355.69 
million providing significant liquidity and financial flexibility. Return on Capital Employed was 45.04% and Return on Equity was 
30.90%, highlighting the strong profitability and efficient capital utilisation.
Recommendation of Dividend 
For FY 2025-26, the Board of Directors has recommended a dividend of H 10.25 per equity share, aggregating to H 1,349.71 million. 
The Company’s dividend distribution is governed by its Dividend Distribution Policy. The dividend is subject to the approval of 
the shareholders at the ensuing Annual General Meeting. Further, the recommendation for FY 2025-26 is consistent with the 
Company’s stated priorities for the uses of cash and after review of its medium-term investment and capex requirements.
Performance of Key Associates and Joint Ventures
The Company’s associates and joint ventures delivered strong growth during FY 2025–26, reflecting increased passenger traffic, 
higher spend per passenger and the ongoing expansion of Airport Travel QSR and Lounge businesses. 
	 Semolina Kitchens Limited generated revenue of H 9,238.93 million, up 47.11% over the previous year, driven by strong 
operating momentum across its multi-airport network and mobilisation of units across the portfolio.
	 GMR Hospitality Limited reported strong revenue growth of 70.13% with revenue from operations (including 
operating income from sub-lease) increasing to H 1,422.56 million, supported by mobilisation of Travel QSR outlets at 
Goa MOPA and Hyderabad airports.
	 Select Service Partner Malaysia Sdn Bhd reported revenue from operations of H 4,183.97 million, registering growth of 32.55% 
year-on-year, reflecting healthy demand across its Travel QSR outlets and Lounge operations at key travel locations in Malaysia.
	 SSP TFS HK Lounge Limited recorded revenue of H 821.00 million, representing a significant YoY increase of 163.14%, driven 
by the recovery and growth of airport lounge operations at Hong Kong International Airport.

Increase in Debtors (Trade Receivables) Days
The increase reflects the launch of the EATS business 
during FY 2025-26. With the business in its ramp-up phase, 
receivables from banks and network partners reflect the built 
up towards the year-end, raising average debtor days. This is 
expected to normalise as the business matures and collection 
cycles stabilise.
Increase in Creditors (Trade Payables) Days
The increase reflects lengthening of the average payment 
cycle during FY 2025-26, as the Company drew on extended 
credit terms with suppliers which was in line with the scaling 
of operations. This supported working capital over the year 
and partially offset the higher debtor days arising from 
the EATS ramp-up. 
Business Outlook 
The Company remains confident about the medium-to-long-
term growth prospects of Indian aviation, driven by low air 
travel penetration, rising disposable incomes, expanding 
airport infrastructure and a continued shift towards 
premium, differentiated travel experiences. These drivers are 
expected to support a 7%-8% passenger traffic CAGR over 
the coming decade.
However, in the near term, the operating environment is 
expected to remain dynamic. The ongoing Middle East conflict 
has impacted international travel sentiment and traffic flows 
on select routes. Alongside this, airline capacity constraints, 
rising inflationary pressures in food commodities, packaging 
and labour, and potential constraints in the availability of raw 
materials remain factors that require close monitoring. These 
pressures have been felt more on international traffic demand, 
though domestic passenger traffic demand remained resilient 
in the first quarter of FY 2026-27. 
For FY 2026-27 (P) the passenger traffic growth is skewed 
towards the second half of the year. International travel is 
expected to take time to recover, but a shift towards domestic 
travel should drive overall demand. The Company continues 
to navigate these near-term challenges supported by strong 
execution, disciplined procurement practices, operational 
efficiencies and a sustained focus on premiumisation. 
Over the medium term, the Company's strategy is anchored 
on two fronts: strengthening its core by driving like-for-like 
sales growth through brand curation and menu innovation, 
while deepening its presence at existing airports and growing 
its network through new concession wins. Alongside this, the 
Company continues to scale its EATS technology platform, 
expand its international lounge footprint across Asia-Pacific 
and the Middle East, and evaluate the long-term expressway 
QSR opportunity.
Capital Allocation Approach
TFS adopts a prudent and strategic approach to capital 
allocation, ensuring that investment decisions are aligned 
with the Company’s growth ambitions and business priorities. 
Capital is directed towards initiatives that improve operational 
resilience, expand market reach, strengthen digital capabilities, 
and build a skilled workforce capable of supporting the evolving 
needs of the business.The Company is well-positioned to fund its medium to long 
term growth aspirations. It remains debt-free, with a strong 
consolidated cash balance and a healthy cash generation 
profile. This financial strength, combined with a disciplined 
approach to capital allocation and a clearly defined set of 
strategic priorities, enables the Company to pursue growth 
opportunities, while navigating ne ar-term uncertainty.
Key Risks and Mitigation Framework
The Company operates in a dynamic business environment 
and is exposed to a range of strategic, operational, financial, 
regulatory and external risks that could affect its business 
performance and operational continuity. These risks arise 
from factors such as macroeconomic conditions, passenger 
traffic trends, evolving customer expectations, partner and 
stakeholder relationships, regulatory developments and the 
broader geopolitical environment. The Company recognises 
that effective identification, assessment and management of 
risks are critical to protecting stakeholder value and supporting 
sustainable long-term growth.
The Company’s performance remains closely linked to traffic 
volumes and airport concession arrangements, exposing it 
to risks relating to aviation cyclicality, geopolitical events, 
airline capacity constraints and fuel cost inflation. Additional 
risks arise from concession renewals, revenue concentration 
across key airports, input cost inflation, and evolving 
regulatory requirements. 
To effectively manage these risks, the Company has 
established an integrated Enterprise Risk Management 
(ERM) framework that enables the systematic identification, 
assessment, monitoring and mitigation of key business 
risks. The framework is overseen by the Risk Management 
Committee of the Board, with clearly defined risk ownership 
across business functions and regular review by senior 
management to ensure that emerging risks are identified 
and appropriate mitigation measures are implemented in a 
timely manner. The internal audit function conducted by an 
external specialist firm, provides objective assurance on the 
design and operating effectiveness of the Company’s internal 
controls. Further details on the Company’s governance 
framework, Enterprise Risk Management framework, 
internal financial controls and oversight mechanisms are 
provided in the Corporate Governance Report forming part 
of this annual report. These measures enable the Company 
to proactively respond to evolving business challenges, 
safeguard stakeholder interests and support sustainable 
long-term value creation.
Human Resources
The Company believes that its people are a key enabler of 
service excellence, operational consistency and sustainable 
growth. Given the customer-facing nature of the travel 
hospitality industry, the Company continues to invest in 
employee capability building, operational training and service 
quality enhancement to deliver consistent guest experiences 
across its businesses.

an Anti-Bribery and Anti-Corruption Policy and a whistle-

audit function that operates on a risk-based annual audit plan, 
covering key processes, locations and functions. Internal audit 
reviews assess the adequacy and effectiveness of internal 
controls and recommend improvements, with management 
taking timely corrective actions. The Audit Committee of the 
Board reviews the internal audit findings, the adequacy and 
effectiveness of the internal control systems, and the status of 
implementation of corrective actions on a periodic basis.
The statutory auditors have also expressed an unmodified 
opinion on the adequacy and operating effectiveness of the 
Company’s internal financial controls over financial reporting. 
The Company continues to review and strengthen its internal 
control framework on an ongoing basis to ensure it remains 
robust and aligned with best practices.

CORPORATE GOVERNANCE PHILOSOPHY
The Company’s governance  framework is central to how we 
conduct our business and create long-term value. Anchored 
in integrity, transparency, accountability, and ethical conduct, 
it promotes responsible decision-making, effective oversight, 
and sustainable value creation for all stakeholders.
Our guiding principles are:
	 Ownership – To lead through accountable performance, 
persistence in overcoming challenges and learning from 
the past while charting new ways forward. 
	 Transparency & Trust – To build trust through unfettered 
transparency, integrity and honesty within our teams, 
with an openness to listen and be heard.
	 People First – To work together in building a culture of 
empathy, meaningful relationships and respect for all the 
people we work with.
These principles help the organisation align with the corporate 
governance philosophy of the Company. The Board of 
Directors of the Company (“the Board”)  play a pivotal role 
in guiding strategic direction, monitoring performance, and 
upholding governance standards. The Board is supported 
by well- structured Committees that provide independent 
oversight in key areas such as audit, risk management, 
nomination and remuneration, and stakeholder relations. 
We strive to maintain timely and transparent disclosure of 
material information and foster a culture of ethical behaviour, 
accountability and risk awareness.
Our commitment to effective governance is reflected in the 
following guiding principles:
	 We are committed to conducting our business fairly, 
ethically, and responsibly, recognising that long-term 
value creation depends on maintaining the trust and 
confidence of our stakeholders, including shareholders, 
employees, customers, business partners, lenders, 
regulators, and the communities in which we operate.
	 We believe that effective governance begins with 
strong leadership. We strive to ensure that leadership 
and oversight roles are entrusted to individuals who demonstrate the highest standards of integrity, 
professional competence, sound judgment, and 
accountability.
	 We continuously strengthen our governance structures, 
internal controls, risk management frameworks, and 
business continuity processes to enhance organisational 
resilience and enable proactive responses to evolving 
market conditions, technological advancements, and 
regulatory developments.
	 We integrate environmental, social, and governance 
(ESG) considerations into our strategy and decision-
making, promoting responsible growth, prudent risk 
management, operational excellence, and sustainable 
value creation for all stakeholders.
	 We foster a culture of transparency, compliance, 
continuous improvement, and ethical conduct 
across the organisation, ensuring that governance 
remains embedded in our business practices and 
strategic objectives.
Our commitment to good governance is continuous and 
evolving. We regularly review and enhance our policies, 
processes, and systems to align with changing regulatory 
requirements, stakeholder expectations, and emerging 
best practices, thereby strengthening our ability to create 
sustainable long-term value.
BOARD OF DIRECTORS
Role and responsibilities of the Board of Directors
The Board is responsible for providing overall strategic 
direction, policy guidance, and ethical oversight to the 
Company, ensuring that operations are conducted in a 
transparent, accountable, and compliant manner. Its key duties 
include formulating long-term strategies and annual business 
plans, monitoring management performance, and ensuring 
the integrity of financial reporting and budgeting processes. 
The Board oversees the Company’s risk management 
framework, internal controls, and compliance systems to 
safeguard assets and uphold regulatory requirements. It also 
maintains an appropriate and balanced composition of skills 
and independence, conducts periodic evaluations of the Board 
and its Committees, and ensures robust succession planning 
for senior leadership. Through these responsibilities, the Board 
promotes sound governance practices and drives sustainable 
value creation for all stakeholders.Report on Corporate Governance

Role of Company Secretary and Compliance Officer
The Company Secretary acts as a key facilitator for the Board 
and ensures its effective functioning by providing appropriate 
guidance on governance practices, applicable laws and 
regulatory requirements. The Company Secretary also acts 
as the Secretary to the Board and its Committees and is 
responsible for convening meetings, preparing agenda papers, 
maintaining accurate records and registers.  
The role includes advising the Board and its members on 
their duties, responsibilities and compliance obligations, and 
supporting the implementation of good corporate governance 
practices. The Company Secretary also serves as an interface 
between the Board, management and regulatory authorities.
In addition, the Company Secretary functions as the 
Compliance Officer and is responsible for ensuring compliance 
with applicable laws, including the Companies Act, 2013 and 
SEBI Listing Regulations.

The Company believes that a well-constituted and diverse 
Board is essential for robust governance and sustainable value 
creation. Diversity in perspectives, professional backgrounds, 
experience, expertise, age and gender enriches Board 
deliberations and enhances its ability to provide strategic 
direction in a dynamic business landscape. Our Directors 
collectively contribute deep knowledge across key domains 
including finance, sectoral leadership, information technology, 
operations, marketing, corporate governance, sustainability, 
risk management, cybersecurity and strategic planning, 
enabling the Company to strengthen its governance framework 
and sustain its competitive advantage.The Company’s Board has an optimum combination of

complies with the regulatory requirements specified in 
the Companies Act, 2013 and SEBI Listing Regulations.

approving the appointment/ re-appointment of Directors 
as may be required from time to time.

the Company’s objectives and values.

SUBSIDIARY COMPLIANCE OVERSIGHT
The Company reviews and monitors the performance of 
its subsidiaries through a robust governance and oversight 
framework, in line with the applicable provisions of the 
Companies Act, 2013 and the SEBI Listing Regulations.
The performance and affairs of the subsidiary companies are 
monitored by the Company in the following manner:
	 The financial statements of subsidiary companies, 
including details of investments made in unlisted 
subsidiaries, are periodically reviewed by the Audit 
Committee of the Company.
	 The minutes of the Board Meetings of unlisted subsidiary 
companies are regularly placed before the Board of 
Directors of the Company for information and review.
	 A statement setting out all significant transactions and 
arrangements entered into by the unlisted subsidiary 
companies is placed before the Board on a periodic basis.
The standalone and consolidated financial statements of the 
subsidiary companies form part of the Company’s consolidated 
financial statements. The financial statements of the unlisted 
subsidiaries are available on the Company’s website at

REMUNERATION OF DIRECTORS
The remuneration of Directors is governed by the Company’s 
Nomination and Remuneration Policy (“NRC Policy”) , 
which is designed to attract, retain and motivate qualified 
and experienced individuals to effectively contribute 
to the Company’s long-term growth, strategy and 
governance objectives. The NRC Policy is available on the 
Company’s website and can be accessed at: https://www.
travelfoodservices.com/tfscms/uploads/governancepolicies/
governancepolicies _1733850494.pdf
The remuneration of Executive Directors comprises fixed and 
variable components, including salary, allowances, perquisites, 
retirement benefits and performance-linked incentives. The 
variable pay component is linked to the achievement of individual 
and Company performance objectives and is determined 
based on financial, operational, strategic and governance 
parameters, as approved by the Board on the recommendation 
of the Nomination and Remuneration Committee. The terms of 
appointment of Executive Directors, including remuneration, 
are approved by the Board and the Shareholders, as applicable, 
in accordance with the provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 
and other applicable laws. No severance fee is payable upon 
termination of the appointment of Executive Directors, except as may be approved by the Shareholders and permissible under 
applicable laws.
The remuneration payable to Non-Executive Directors, including 
Independent Directors, is determined by the Board based on 
the recommendations of the Nomination and Remuneration 
Committee, having regard to their roles and responsibilities, Board 
and Committee memberships, time commitment, experience, 
expertise and contribution to the Company’s governance and 
strategic oversight. Non-Executive Directors are paid sitting fees 
for attending meetings of the Board and its Committees within 
the limits prescribed under applicable laws and as approved by 
the Board. In addition, the Company may pay commission to Non-
Executive Directors, subject to the approval of the Shareholders 
and within the limits prescribed under applicable laws.
Independent Directors are not entitled to stock options or any 
other form of equity-linked or performance-based incentive 
compensation. Details of stock options granted to Executive 
Directors, if any, under the Company’s Employee Stock Option 
Scheme are disclosed in the Directors’ Report and the financial 
statements. Such options vest and become exercisable in 
accordance with the terms of the relevant scheme approved by 
the Shareholders and applicable regulatory requirements.

BOARD COMMITTEES
In compliance with the provisions of the Companies Act, 2013, the Rules made thereunder and the SEBI Listing Regulations, the 
Board has constituted various statutory committees to assist in the effective discharge of its responsibilities and to strengthen the 
Company’s corporate governance framework.
The Committees comprise Directors with the requisite skills, expertise and experience and function within the authority delegated 
by the Board. They provide focused oversight on key matters to support effective decision-making, accountability, and governance. 
The Chairperson of each Committee apprises the Board of significant discussions, recommendations and decisions taken at the 
respective Committee meetings. The minutes of all Committee meetings are placed before the Board for its review and noting. 
During the year under review, all recommendations made by the Committees of the Board have been accepted by the Board.

backgrounds, having due regard to diversity; and

Share Transfer System:
As mandated by the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI), securities of the Company can be transferred/traded only in 
dematerialised form. Accordingly, as on March 31, 2026, 100% of the shareholding of the Company is held in dematerialised form 
with the depositories, namely National Securities Depository Limited (NSDL) and Central Depository Services (India) Limited (CDSL).
The Company has appointed MUFG Intime India Private Limited (formerly known as Link Intime India Private Limited) , the RTA 
to handle all activities relating to share registry work. All requests for transmission, transposition, dividend payment processes, 
grievance redressal and other shareholder related services are carried out by the RTA in coordination with the Company.

POLICIES AND COMPANY’S CODE OF CONDUCT
In compliance with the requirements of the Companies Act, 
2013, the SEBI Listing Regulations and relevant sustainability 
frameworks, the Company has implemented several policies and 
codes. An overview of the key policies is provided in this section.
Policy on Related Party Transactions 
The Company has adopted a Policy on Related Party 
Transactions (‘RPT Policy’).  
The RPT Policy lays down guidelines for:
	 Identification of related parties and related party 
transactions in accordance with applicable laws and 
accounting standards.
	 Determining the materiality of related party transactions 
based on prescribed thresholds.
	 Establishing a framework for prior approval, review, and 
ratification of related party transactions by the Audit 
Committee and the Board, and where required, by 
the shareholders.
	 Ensuring that related party transactions are entered into 
in the ordinary course of business and on an arm’s length 
basis, and are conducted in a transparent manner.
	 Proper documentation, reporting, and disclosure of related 
party transactions to ensure compliance with regulatory 
requirements and to prevent conflicts of interest.
The Policy aims to safeguard the interests of the Company 
and its shareholders by ensuring integrity, transparency, 
and fairness in all related party dealings. The same can 
be accessed on the Company’s website at https://www.
travelfoodservices.com/tfscms/uploads/governancepolicies/
governancepolicies _1762249208.pdfCode of conduct for the Board and Senior Management 
Personnel
The Company has adopted the Code of Conduct for the 
Board of Directors and Senior Management (“the Code”) in 
accordance with the SEBI Listing Regulations.
The Code lays down the principles, standards and ethical 
framework governing the conduct of the Directors and Senior 
Management personnel and seeks to ensure that the Company 
conducts its business with integrity, transparency and in 
compliance with applicable laws and regulations.
The Code:
	 Applies to all Directors, including executive, 
non-executive and independent directors, and to 
members of Senior Management as defined under the 
SEBI Listing Regulations.
	 Establishes standards of ethical behaviour, legal 
compliance, accountability and fair dealings 
with stakeholders.
	 Aims to prevent conflicts of interest and promote honest 
and ethical conduct in all business activities.
	 Emphasises safeguarding of Company assets, 
confidentiality of information and adherence to corporate 
governance principles.
The Code forms an integral part of the Company’s corporate 
governance framework and underscores its commitment to 
ethical business practices and responsible leadership.
The Code is available on the Company’s website and can be 
accessed at https://www.travelfoodservices.com/tfscms/uploads/
governancepolicies/governancepolicies_1733850779.pdf . 
All the Board members and Senior Management Personnel 
have affirmed compliance with the Code for the financial year 
ended March 31, 2026. A declaration to this effect, signed by 
the Managing Director & CEO forms part of this Report.
Vigil Mechanism and Whistle Blower / Speak Up Policy
The Vigil Mechanism and Whistle Blower / Speak Up Policy 
of the Company are detailed in the Directors’ Report forming

Code of Conduct for Prevention of Insider Trading
Pursuant to the provisions of the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India (Prohibition of Insider Trading) Regulations, 
2015 (“SEBI PIT Regulations” ), as amended, the Company has 
in place a Code of Conduct for Prevention of Insider Trading 
(“Code”) and a Code of Practices and Procedures for Fair 
Disclosure of Unpublished Price Sensitive Information (“UPSI” )  
(“Code for Fair Disclosure”) 
The Code is designed to regulate, monitor and report trading 
by Designated Persons and their immediate relatives and to 
preserve the confidentiality of UPSI. The Code is applicable 
to Directors, Designated Persons and such other persons who 
may have access to UPSI pertaining to the Company.

Disclosure of commodity price risks and commodity 
hedging activities.
The Company operates in the Quick Service Restaurant (QSR) 
sector and is exposed to fluctuations in the prices of certain 
food ingredients, packaging materials, and related inputs that 
are sourced in the ordinary course of business. These price 
variations are managed through established procurement 
practices, including vendor diversification, negotiated supply 
arrangements, and periodic price reviews. The Company 
follows a prudent sourcing strategy and relies on operational 
efficiencies, menu engineering, and cost optimisation 
measures to mitigate the impact of commodity price volatility.
The Company does not engage in any commodity hedging 
transactions, including the use of derivatives or other financial 
instruments, for managing commodity price risks. Accordingly, 
disclosure of commodity hedging activities is not applicable 
to the Company.
The Board periodically reviews key input cost trends as 
part of its overall risk management framework to ensure 
that any material impact on operations is appropriately 
monitored and addressed.

allotment or qualified institutions placement as specified

allotment or qualified institutions placement during the 
financial year and, accordingly, the disclosure requirements 
under Regulation 32(7A) of the SEBI Listing Regulations are 
not applicable.

Disclosure of certain types of agreements binding 
listed entities- Information disclosed under clause 5A 
of paragraph A of Part A of Schedule III of SEBI Listing 
Regulations.
The promoters of the Company, namely SSP Asia Pacific 
Holdings Limited (“SSP”) , Kapur Family Trust (“KFT”) , 
Mr. Varun Kapur and Mr. Karan Kapur, have entered into 
an Inter-se Agreement dated December 9, 2024 (“Inter-
se Agreement”). Pursuant to the Inter-se Agreement, 
for so long as each of SSP and KFT individually holds at 
least 20% of the equity share capital of the Company and 
continues to be classified as a promoter of the Company, 
SSP and KFT have agreed that, in relation to certain 
specified matters requiring shareholder approval, including  
(i) changes in the share capital of the Company or its 
subsidiaries, including by way of corporate restructuring, 
(ii) divestment of subsidiaries or investments, (iii) entry into 
joint ventures, profit-sharing arrangements, establishment of 
subsidiaries or commencement or cessation of businesses, 
and (iv) employee option, compensation or incentive 
arrangements, they shall seek to mutually agree their 
voting position prior to casting their votes and shall vote 
in accordance with such mutual agreement, subject to 
applicable law.
The Inter-se Agreement further records that voting on any 
other matter remains an independent commercial decision of 
each party acting in its own interest and that the arrangement 
is not intended to be construed as creating a persons acting 
in concert relationship.
The salient terms of the Inter-se Agreement were disclosed 
in the Prospectus dated July 10, 2025, available at www.
travelf oodservices.com

 b. What is the contribution of exports as a percentage of the total turnover of the entity?
The Company is a leading player in India’s fast growing airport Travel Quick Service Restaurant (“Travel QSR”) 
and Lounge sectors. It operates a diversified portfolio of partner and in-house brands, serving travellers across 
key airport locations. The Company’s business model is strengthened by long-standing relationships with

QSRs and lounges, we cater to the evolving needs of travellers seeking quality, convenience, and comfort.

7. Statement by director responsible for the business 
responsibility report, highlighting ESG related challenges, 
targets and achievements (listed entity has flexibility 
regarding the placement of this disclosure)At Travel Food Services, we recognise that operating at the 
heart of India’s travel ecosystem places a unique responsibility 
on us to serve millions of passengers in a manner that is safe, 
inclusive, and environmentally responsible. We are mindful of 
the ESG challenges inherent to our business, including energy 
and emissions intensity, reliance on packaging, food waste, 
water usage, and the need to nurture a diverse workforce. In 
response, we have set clear priorities around transitioning to 
cleaner energy, eliminating single-use plastics, conserving water, 
reducing food waste, advancing gender diversity, and upholding 
the highest standards of food safety and governance. Guided by 
these commitments, we will continue to embed sustainability 
across our value chain and create enduring value for our 
travellers, partners, employees, and the communities we serve.

SECTION: PRINCIPLE WISE PERFORMANCE DISCLOSURE C
This section provides an overview of Travel Food Services Limited’s commitment to integrating the NGRBC Principles and Core 
Elements into its key business processes and decision-making frameworks. The disclosure requirements are presented under two 
categories for each principle: “Essential” and “Leadership.” While the essential indicators are mandatory, the leadership indicators 
are voluntary and reflect the Company’s broader commitment to responsible business conduct. Through its disclosures under the 
Essential indicators, Travel Food Services Limited highlights its commitment to sustainable and responsible business practices. 
The reporting reflects the Company’s approach to creating long-term value while upholding social, environmental, and ethical 
responsibilities across its operations in the Travel QSR and Lounge sector.

Principle 1 Businesses should conduct and govern themselves with integrity, and in a 
manner that is ethical, transparent, and accountable. 
 
At Travel Food Services Limited, strong governance forms the foundation of sustainable and responsible business operations. 
To promote ethical conduct, transparency and accountability across all levels of the organization, the Company follows a robust 
framework of policies and practices that guide its operations and support long-term business continuity. To uphold high standards 
of professional and ethical behavior, the Company has adopted a Code of Conduct applicable to the Board of Directors and Senior 
Management, along with an internal Code of Conduct and Ethics policy for employees. In addition, employee guidelines and 
internal policies are communicated across the organization to encourage responsible business behavior and ethical engagement 
with both internal and external stakeholders.

Principle 2 Businesses should provide goods and services in a manner that is  
sustainable and safe.
Travel Food Services Limited is committed to delivering quality food, beverage, and hospitality services to its customers through 
responsible, safe, and sustainable business practices. As a prominent player in the QSR and Lounge sector, the Company carefully 
curates its offerings and collaborates with partners and brands that align with its standards for quality, safety, and responsible 
operations. By upholding these principles, Travel Food Services Limited ensures that customers receive a diverse and reliable range 
of products and services that meet evolving consumer preferences while reflecting the Company’s commitment to sustainability 
and operational excellence. The Company remains focused on enhancing the overall customer experience while supporting 
sustainable practices.

2. a. Does the entity have procedures in place for sustainable sourcing? (Yes/No)
Yes. The entity defines sustainable sourcing expectations in its Supplier Code of Conduct covering human rights, 
product quality and food safety, environmental sustainability and business conduct. We expect 100% of our contracted 
suppliers to sign up to our Supplier Code of Conduct or demonstrate equal standards. We also have key metrics and 
targets in place for applicable suppliers relating to food safety and certifications for certain products.
 b. If yes, what percentage of inputs were sourced sustainably? 
  Yes, the following were sourced sustainably:
  1) 100% of our contracted suppliers have signed up to our Supplier Code or demonstrated equal standards;
  2) 100% of applicable standards meet our FSSAI food safety standards; 
  3) 83% of tea for our own brands is sourced from Rainforest Alliance certified farms; 
  4) 89% of fish for our own brands is from fisheries certified to standards such as Marine Stewardship council (MSC) or 
classified as ‘Least Concern’ by the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) Red List; and 
  5) 100% of our packaging is free from unnecessary single use plastic and recyclable, reusable or compostable.

 The Company is currently assessing its waste streams. As part of this exercise, it is undertaking waste inventorisation and

Principle 3 Businesses should respect and promote the well-being of all employees, 
including those in their value chains. 
Travel Food Services Limited is committed to providing a safe, secure, and healthy workplace for its employees, vendors, partners, 
and others across its value chain, while upholding high standards of occupational health and safety. The Company places strong 
emphasis on the well-being of its workforce and actively promotes awareness and engagement on health and safety matters. It 
is also committed to fostering an inclusive and diverse workplace by ensuring equal opportunity and maintaining an environment 
free from discrimination across its operations.

10. Health and safety management system: 
 a. Whether an occupational health and safety management system has been implemented by the entity?  
(Yes/ No). If yes, what is the coverage of such a system?
Yes. We have implemented an Occupational Health and Safety (OH&S) Management System aligned with applicable 
statutory requirements and globally recognised standards such as ISO 45001. The system covers 100% of the Company's 
employees, and contract staff across all operational locations, including airport and highway outlets, central kitchens, 
warehouses, and corporate offices, encompassing aspects such as kitchen safety, fire safety, ergonomic practices, food 
handling hygiene, incident reporting and regular health & safety training. 
STATUTORY REPORTS
145
Annual Report 2025-26
=== page 120 ===
 b. What are the processes used to identify work-related hazards and assess risks on a routine and non-routine 
basis by the entity? 
We have established a structured Hazard Identification and Risk Assessment (HIRA) framework across all our outlets, 
central kitchens, and lounges to ensure a safe and healthy working environment.
For routine operations, hazards are systematically identified through daily safety checklists, periodic internal safety 
audits, and regular kitchen and equipment inspections. For non-routine activities such as equipment installation, 
deep cleaning, maintenance work, and new outlet commissioning work permits are issued prior to commencement, 
supported by active on-site supervision.
All identified hazards and associated risks are documented and reviewed by the Occupational Health and Safety 
(OHS) team. Appropriate mitigation measures are implemented through a hierarchy of controls, including engineering 
controls, standardized operating procedures (SOPs), use of personal protective equipment (PPE) and focused employee 
training programs.
 c. Whether you have processes for workers to report the work-related hazards and to remove themselves from 
such risks. (Y/N)
Multiple channels are available for the workforce to report work-related hazards, unsafe conditions and near-miss 
incidents. These include informing immediate supervisors, raising concerns during routine safety audits and inspections, 
utilizing formal incident and near-miss reporting systems. Reported concerns are promptly investigated and addressed 
through corrective actions. Employees are trained and empowered to remove themselves from any situation posing an 
imminent risk to their health or safety.
 d. Do the employees/ workers of the entity have access to non-occupational medical and healthcare services? 
(Yes/ No)
Yes, the employees are covered under medical and accidental insurance schemes.

12. Describe the measures taken by the entity to ensure a safe and healthy workplace. 
TFS is committed to providing a safe, hygienic, and healthy workplace across all its outlets, central kitchens and lounges. 
We have implemented a structured Occupational Health & Safety (OH&S) framework supported by the following measures:
	 Policies & Systems:  A documented OHS policy and standard operating procedures (SOPs)
	 Hazard Identification:  Risk Assessment, kitchen and equipment inspections and internal safety audits to proactively 
identify and mitigate risks.
	 Training & Awareness: Regular safety induction, toolbox talks, fire safety drills, first-aid training and refresher programs 
for its workforce.
	 Infrastructure & PPE: Provision of appropriate personal protective equipment (gloves, non-slip footwear, aprons, heat-
resistant gear), well-maintained kitchen equipment, fire-fighting systems and emergency exits at all locations.
	 Health & Hygiene: Group medical insurance, hygiene protocols, pest control clean drinking water and sanitation facilities.
	 Incident Management: A robust incident reporting and investigation mechanism with root-cause analysis and corrective/
preventive actions to prevent recurrence.
These initiatives collectively foster a culture of safety, well-being and continuous improvement across the organization.

its stakeholders.
Travel Food Services Limited recognizes that meaningful stakeholder engagement is integral to its operational effectiveness and 
long-term sustainability across the value chain. The Company is committed to understanding and addressing the expectations and 
concerns of its key stakeholders, including employees, customers, business partners, shareholders, investors, regulatory authorities 
and the communities in which it operates. To support this approach, the Company has established appropriate grievance redressal 
mechanisms for relevant stakeholder groups, including customers, employees and shareholders, enabling timely and effective 
resolution of concerns and reinforcing trust, transparency and stakeholder confidence.
Essential Indicators  
1. Describe the processes for identifying key stakeholder groups of the entity. 
 TFS identifies key stakeholder groups through a structured assessment of individuals and institutions impacted by or 
influencing its operations. The process includes mapping internal and external stakeholders such as employees, customers, 
suppliers, investors, regulators, communities and business partners. Stakeholders are prioritized based on their level of 
influence, dependency, engagement frequency and relevance to business sustainability. Inputs from consultations, business 
reviews, regulatory requirements and grievance mechanisms are used to periodically review and update stakeholder groups.

Principle 5Businesses should respect and promote human rights
Travel Food Services Limited is committed to upholding human rights and labour rights across its operations and value chain. The 
Company’s governance framework and Code of Conduct set out clear expectations on key matters, including the prohibition of 
child labour and forced labour, prevention of discrimination and harassment and the promotion of diversity, equity, and inclusion. 
The Company has also instituted a whistleblower mechanism to encourage employees and stakeholders to report concerns 
relating to unethical conduct, misconduct, or violations of the Code, without fear of retaliation. The relevant reporting channels 
are made available through the Company’s established communication platforms, reinforcing its commitment to accountability, 
transparency and responsible business conduct.

Yes, the Human Resources (HR) function serves as the focal point responsible for addressing human rights impacts or issues 
caused or contributed to by the business.
5. Describe the internal mechanisms in place to redress grievances related to human rights issues. 
TFS has internal grievance redressal mechanisms managed by the Human Resources (HR) function, which acts as the focal 
point for addressing human rights-related concerns. Employees can raise grievances relating to workplace discrimination, 
harassment, unfair treatment, health and safety, wages, working conditions, or any other human rights concerns.
Grievances are reviewed by HR in a confidential and impartial manner, with appropriate investigation and corrective action 
taken wherever required. The Company also has mechanisms such as employee grievance handling processes, escalation 
to senior management where necessary, and applicable committees such as the Internal Committee for prevention and 
redressal of sexual harassment complaints. The Company endeavours to ensure that grievances are addressed in a timely 
manner and that complainants are protected against retaliation.

accordance with its established processes. At highway locations, wastewater is treated through the respective Effluent

Principle 9 Businesses should engage with and provide value to their consumers in a 
responsible manner. 
Travel Food Services Limited recognizes the evolving preferences of consumers for more responsible and sustainable products 
and services and is progressively integrating sustainable practices into its operations and customer offerings. This approach not 
only strengthens the Company’s environmental commitment but also aligns with changing customer expectations within the QSR 
and Lounge services sectors. The Company also places importance on customer feedback and continuously seeks to enhance the 
service experience and improve customer satisfaction across its operations.
Essential Indicators
1. Describe the mechanisms in place to receive and respond to consumer complaints and feedback.
TFS has instituted a robust Customer Experience and Complaint Management Framework to ensure 
timely, transparent, and effective resolution of consumer complaints and feedback.
	 Dedicated customer service email: customerservice@travelf oodservices.com
	 24x7 Contact Centre helpline: 9867222111
	 QR code-based survey feedback at outlets
	 Social media platforms (Twitter, Instagram, Facebook)
	 Feedback escalations received via airport and highway authorities
	 All complaints are logged and tracked through an integrated CRM system 
managed by the Contact Centre team.
	 A Service Champion coordinates with regional teams and site managers to 
investigate the issue, obtain incident reports, CCTV footage, and conduct Root 
Cause Analysis (RCA).
	 Final closure communication is issued by the Customer Experience Head/
Manager at the Head Office.
	 A defined Turnaround Time (TAT) relating to the specific nature of complaint is 
maintained for complaint closure, supported by a structured Escalation Matrix 
extending up to senior leadership.
	 Daily dashboards on region-wise complaints are circulated to 
internal stakeholders.
	 Weekly analyses are shared to drive Corrective and Preventive Actions (CAPA).
	 Specific issues are escalated to the relevant functions, such as Training & HR 
(staff-related), Executive Chef (food-related), Supply Chain, and Maintenance 
teams, with timelines and evidence-based closures.Multiple Channels for Receiving Feedback:
Complaint Handling and Resolution Process:
Monitoring, Reporting, and Continuous Improvement:
This end-to-end mechanism ensures that consumer voices are heard, addressed 
promptly, and leveraged for continuous enhancement of service quality and 
customer satisfaction.

Independent Auditor’s Report
To 
The Members of

Report on the Audit of the Standalone Financial 
Statements
Opinion
We have audited the standalone financial statements of 
Travel Food Services Limited (formerly known as Travel Food 
Services Private Limited) (the “Company”) which comprise 
the standalone balance sheet as at 31 March 2026, and the 
standalone statement of profit and loss (including other 
comprehensive income), standalone statement of changes in 
equity and the standalone statement of cash flows for the year 
then ended, and notes to the standalone financial statements, 
including material accounting policies and other explanatory 
information (hereinafter referred to as "Standalone 
Financial Statements")
In our opinion and to the best of our information and according 
to the explanations given to us, the aforesaid standalone 
financial statements give the information required by the 
Companies Act, 2013 (“Act”) in the manner so required and 
give a true and fair view in conformity with the accounting 
principles generally accepted in India, of the state of affairs of 
the Company as at 31 March 2026, and its profit and other 
comprehensive loss, changes in equity and its cash flows for 
the year ended on that date.Basis for Opinion
We conducted our audit in accordance with the Standards 
on Auditing (SAs) specified under Section 143(10) of the Act. 
Our responsibilities under those SAs are further described in 
the Auditor’s Responsibilities for the Audit of the Standalone 
Financial Statements section of our report. We are independent 
of the Company in accordance with the Code of Ethics issued by 
the Institute of Chartered Accountants of India together with 
the ethical requirements that are relevant to our audit of the 
standalone financial statements under the provisions of the 
Act and the Rules thereunder, and we have fulfilled our other 
ethical responsibilities in accordance with these requirements 
and the Code of Ethics. We believe that the audit evidence we 
have obtained is sufficient and appropriate to provide a basis 
for our opinion on the standalone financial statements.
Key Audit Matter
Key audit matters are those matters that, in our professional 
judgment, were of most significance in our audit of the standalone 
financial statements of the current period. These matters were 
addressed in the context of our audit of the standalone financial 
statements as a whole, and in forming our opinion thereon, and 
we do not provide a separate opinion on these matters.
Revenue from operations
See notes 2B(i)(a) and 26 to standalone financial statements
The key audit matter How the matter was addressed in our audit
The Company is primarily involved in managing and operating 
food and beverage outlets (Travel Quick Service Restaurant 
(QSR)) and Lounge Services within domestic and international 
airports.
The accounting policies of the Company for the different 
revenue streams are set out in note 2B(i)(a) to the standalone 
financial statements. 
The Company and its external stakeholders focus on revenue 
as a key performance indicator and there exists the risk 
of misstatement of the timing and existence of revenue 
recognized. There is also a risk that the revenue may be 
recognized inappropriately for sales made through QSR 
outlets and at airport lounge.Our audit procedures in respect of this matter included the  
following:
	 Evaluated the appropriateness of the Company’s 
accounting policies for revenue recognition in accordance 
with the applicable accounting framework.
	 Obtained an understanding and tested design, 
implementation and on sample basis, the operating 
effectiveness of the key internal financial controls 
relating to Lounge and QSR revenue recognition process.
	 Performed substantive testing on statistically selected 
samples of revenue transactions recorded during the year 
by verifying the relevant underlying documents including 
invoices, agreements, customer acknowledgement, etc. 
and cut-off testing around the reporting date to assess 
the completeness and existence of revenue recognition.

whether due to fraud or error.

assessing the Company’s ability to continue as a going concern, 
disclosing, as applicable, matters related to going concern and 
using the going concern basis of accounting unless the Board 
of Directors either intends to liquidate the Company or to 
cease operations, or has no realistic alternative but to do so. 
The Board of Directors is also responsible for overseeing the 
Company’s financial reporting process.
Auditor’s Responsibilities for the Audit of the 
Standalone Financial Statements
Our objectives are to obtain reasonable assurance about 
whether the standalone financial statements as a whole 
are free from material misstatement, whether due to fraud 
or error, and to issue an auditor’s report that includes our 
opinion. Reasonable assurance is a high level of assurance, 
but is not a guarantee that an audit conducted in accordance 
with SAs will always detect a material misstatement when it 
exists. Misstatements can arise from fraud or error and are 
considered material if, individually or in the aggregate, they 
could reasonably be expected to influence the economic 
decisions of users taken on the basis of these standalone 
financial statements.
As part of an audit in accordance with SAs, we exercise 
professional judgment and maintain professional skepticism 
throughout the audit. We also:
	 Identify and assess the risks of material misstatement of 
the standalone financial statements, whether due to fraud 
or error, design and perform audit procedures responsive 
to those risks, and obtain audit evidence that is sufficient 
and appropriate to provide a basis for our opinion. The risk 
of not detecting a material misstatement resulting from 
fraud is higher than for one resulting from error, as fraud 
may involve collusion, forgery, intentional omissions, 
misrepresentations, or the override of internal control.Independent Auditor’s Report (continued)

Annexure B to the Independent Auditor’s Report  
on the standalone financial statements of Travel Food Services Limited (formerly known as Travel Food Services Private 
Limited) for the year ended 31 March 2026
Report on the internal financial controls with reference to the aforesaid standalone financial statements under Clause (i) of 
Sub-section 3 of Section 143 of the Act
(Referred to in paragraph 2(A)(g) under ‘Report on Other Legal and Regulatory Requirements’ section of our report of even date)
Opinion
We have audited the internal financial controls with reference 
to financial statements of Travel Food Services Limited 
(formerly known as Travel Food Services Private Limited) (“the 
Company”) as of 31 March 2026 in conjunction with our audit 
of the standalone financial statements of the Company for the 
year ended on that date.
In our opinion, the Company has, in all material respects, 
adequate internal financial controls with reference to financial 
statements and such internal financial controls were operating 
effectively as at 31 March 2026, based on the internal 
financial controls with reference to financial statements 
criteria established by the Company considering the essential 
components of internal control stated in the Guidance Note on 
Audit of Internal Financial Controls Over Financial Reporting 
issued by the Institute of Chartered Accountants of India (the 
“Guidance Note”).
Management’s and Board of Directors’ 
Responsibilities for Internal Financial Controls
The Company’s Management and the Board of Directors are 
responsible for establishing and maintaining internal financial 
controls based on the internal financial controls with reference 
to financial statements criteria established by the Company 
considering the essential components of internal control 
stated in the Guidance Note. These responsibilities include 
the design, implementation and maintenance of adequate 
internal financial controls that were operating effectively for 
ensuring the orderly and efficient conduct of its business, 
including adherence to company’s policies, the safeguarding of 
its assets, the prevention and detection of frauds and errors, 
the accuracy and completeness of the accounting records, 
and the timely preparation of reliable financial information, as 
required under the Act.
Auditor’s Responsibility
Our responsibility is to express an opinion on the Company’s 
internal financial controls with reference to financial statements 
based on our audit. We conducted our audit in accordance with 
the Guidance Note and the Standards on Auditing, prescribed 
under Section 143(10) of the Act, to the extent applicable to an audit of internal financial controls with reference to 
financial statements. Those Standards and the Guidance Note 
require that we comply with ethical requirements and plan 
and perform the audit to obtain reasonable assurance about 
whether adequate internal financial controls with reference to 
financial statements were established and maintained and if 
such controls operated effectively in all material respects.
Our audit involves performing procedures to obtain audit 
evidence about the adequacy of the internal financial controls 
with reference to financial statements and their operating 
effectiveness. Our audit of internal financial controls with 
reference to financial statements included obtaining an 
understanding of internal financial controls with reference 
to financial statements, assessing the risk that a material 
weakness exists, and testing and evaluating the design and 
operating effectiveness of internal control based on the 
assessed risk. The procedures selected depend on the auditor’s 
judgement, including the assessment of the risks of material 
misstatement of the standalone financial statements, whether 
due to fraud or error.
We believe that the audit evidence we have obtained is 
sufficient and appropriate to provide a basis for our audit 
opinion on the Company’s internal financial controls with 
reference to financial statements.
Meaning of Internal Financial Controls with Reference 
to Financial Statements
A company's internal financial controls with reference 
to financial statements is a process designed to provide 
reasonable assurance regarding the reliability of financial 
reporting and the preparation of financial statements for 
external purposes in accordance with generally accepted 
accounting principles. A company's internal financial controls 
with reference to financial statements include those policies 
and procedures that (1) pertain to the maintenance of records 
that, in reasonable detail, accurately and fairly reflect the 
transactions and dispositions of the assets of the company; (2) 
provide reasonable assurance that transactions are recorded 
as necessary to permit preparation of financial statements in 
accordance with generally accepted accounting principles, and 
that receipts and expenditures of the company are being made 
Travel Food Services Limited  
168

=== page 143 ===
only in accordance with authorisations of management and 
directors of the company; and (3) provide reasonable assurance 
regarding prevention or timely detection of unauthorised 
acquisition, use, or disposition of the company's assets that 
could have a material effect on the financial statements.
Inherent Limitations of Internal Financial Controls 
with Reference to Financial Statements
Because of the inherent limitations of internal financial 
controls with reference to financial statements, including the 
possibility of collusion or improper management override 
of controls, material misstatements due to error or fraud 
may occur and not be detected. Also, projections of any 
evaluation of the internal financial controls with reference to 
financial statements to future periods are subject to the risk that the internal financial controls with reference to financial 
statements may become inadequate because of changes in 
conditions, or that the degree of compliance with the policies 
or procedures may deteriorate.
